2024 Mexican general election

General elections were held in [Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico "Mexico") on 2 June 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-5-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-5)[&#8202;](#ref-back-6-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-6) Voters elected a new [president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Mexico "President of Mexico") to serve a six-year term, all 500 members of the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_(Mexico) "Chamber of Deputies (Mexico)"), and all 128 members of the [Senate of the Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Republic_(Mexico) "Senate of the Republic (Mexico)"). These elections took place concurrently with the [2024 state elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Mexican_local_elections "2024 Mexican local elections").

[Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Sheinbaum "Claudia Sheinbaum"), a member of the [left-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-wing "Left-wing") political party [Morena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morena_(political_party) "Morena (political party)"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-7-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-7) was widely regarded by her party as the top contender to succeed President [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador "Andrés Manuel López Obrador") and ultimately secured the nomination of the ruling coalition, [Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia"). [Xóchitl Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%B3chitl_G%C3%A1lvez "Xóchitl Gálvez") emerged as the frontrunner of [Fuerza y Corazón por México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuerza_y_Coraz%C3%B3n_por_M%C3%A9xico "Fuerza y Corazón por México") following a surge in popularity due to criticisms from López Obrador.[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-9) [Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens'_Movement_(Mexico) "Citizens' Movement (Mexico)"), the only national party without a coalition, nominated [Jorge Máynez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_M%C3%A1ynez "Jorge Máynez"). This was the first general election in Mexico's history in which most contenders for the country's presidency were women.

Sheinbaum won the presidential election by a landslide margin of over 33 points, becoming the first woman and the first person of [Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Jews_in_Mexico "History of the Jews in Mexico") descent to be elected president of Mexico. She was also the first Jewish woman elected head of state in Latin America.[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-11) The election saw Sheinbaum receiving the highest number of votes ever recorded for a candidate in Mexican history, surpassing López Obrador's record of 30.1 million votes [from 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Mexican_general_election "2018 Mexican general election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-:13) Sheinbaum was officially sworn into office on 1 October 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-13-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-13)

In the legislative elections, the [Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia") coalition won a [supermajority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermajority "Supermajority") in the Chamber of Deputies, securing 73% of the seats, the highest share for a party or coalition since [1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Mexican_general_election "1982 Mexican general election").[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-14) The alliance fell three seats short of a supermajority in the Senate, but defections by the two senators elected for the PRD on 28 August closed the shortfall to one; the supermajority was ultimately secured with the defection to Morena of [Cynthia López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_L%C3%B3pez_Castro "Cynthia López Castro"), elected for the PRI in Mexico City, on 12 November.[&#8202;](#ref-back-brujula-lopez-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-brujula-lopez)

## Background

### Political background

In the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Mexican_general_election "2018 Mexican general election"), [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador "Andrés Manuel López Obrador"), a three-time presidential candidate and founder of the [National Regeneration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morena_(political_party) "Morena (political party)") (Morena), ran as the candidate of the [Juntos Haremos Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juntos_Haremos_Historia "Juntos Haremos Historia") alliance, which included Morena, the [Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Party_(Mexico) "Labor Party (Mexico)") (PT), and the [Social Encounter Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Encounter_Party "Social Encounter Party") (PES). He won in a landslide, securing 54.71% of the vote and defeating his closest rival, [Ricardo Anaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Anaya "Ricardo Anaya") of the [Por México al Frente](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Por_M%C3%A9xico_al_Frente "Por México al Frente") coalition, by nearly 31 percentage points. The Juntos Haremos Historia alliance also secured majorities in the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_(Mexico) "Chamber of Deputies (Mexico)") and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Republic_(Mexico) "Senate of the Republic (Mexico)").

As president, López Obrador expanded the Mexican welfare state with programs aimed at the elderly and low-income students, raised the minimum wage significantly, and invested heavily in infrastructure projects such as the [Tren Maya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tren_Maya "Tren Maya") and the [Felipe Ángeles International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_%C3%81ngeles_International_Airport "Felipe Ángeles International Airport").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:21-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-:21) He combatted corruption by implementing [austerity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austerity "Austerity") measures, including salary caps, budget cuts across the government, and the dismissal of numerous government employees.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-17)[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-18) While his administration achieved a poverty reduction, it faced accusations of [democratic backsliding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_backsliding "Democratic backsliding") and undermining institutional independence, including frequent attacks on journalists and judges during his morning press conferences and questioning the necessity of independent public agencies such as the INE and INAI.[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-19) The country’s murder rate fell from 30 per 100,000 people in 2018 when AMLO entered office, to 28 per 100,000 people in 2021 according to [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank") data. Between 2019 and 2023 there was a 15 percent decrease in the number of murders according to public data.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:21-1)[\[14\]](#refnote-:21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-20)

During the [2021 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Mexican_legislative_election "2021 Mexican legislative election"), party alliances shifted significantly. The [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_(Mexico) "National Action Party (Mexico)") (PAN), the [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party "Institutional Revolutionary Party") (PRI), and the [Party of the Democratic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Democratic_Revolution "Party of the Democratic Revolution") (PRD) formed [a big-tent coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Va_por_M%C3%A9xico "Va por México") to challenge Morena's growing dominance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-22) While [Juntos Hacemos Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juntos_Hacemos_Historia "Juntos Hacemos Historia"), an alliance of Morena, the PT, and the [Ecologist Green Party of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecologist_Green_Party_of_Mexico "Ecologist Green Party of Mexico") (PVEM), lost its [supermajority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermajority "Supermajority") in the Chamber of Deputies, it achieved significant success in [local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_Mexican_local_elections "2021 Mexican local elections"), flipping numerous governorships.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-24)[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-25)

López Obrador remained highly popular throughout his presidency, consistently maintaining a 60% approval rating, making him one of the most popular leaders globally.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:21-2)[\[14\]](#refnote-:21)[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-26)

### Political violence

Further information: [List of politicians killed during the 2024 Mexican elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_politicians_killed_during_the_2024_Mexican_elections "List of politicians killed during the 2024 Mexican elections")

Political violence in Mexico typically escalates during election season, with candidates often facing threats, abductions, assaults, or assassinations. While this violence is most common at the municipal level, it can also occur at the federal level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-27)

During the 2021 elections, 102 politicians were killed, including 36 who were nominees or candidates for public office.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-28)

By January 2024, before the campaigning period started, multiple aspiring candidates for political office had been killed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-29) The government provided security guards to around 560 candidates and election officials.[&#8202;](#ref-back-aj37-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-aj37) Around 27,000 [Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Armed_Forces "Mexican Armed Forces") and [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_(Mexico) "National Guard (Mexico)") personnel were deployed to secure the electoral process.[&#8202;](#ref-back-31-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-31) By May 2024, the candidate death toll had risen to 37.[&#8202;](#ref-back-aj37-1)[\[28\]](#refnote-aj37)

## Electoral system

### Presidency

The president is elected by [plurality voting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_voting "Plurality voting") in a single round; there is no provision for a second run-off round.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-32) Article 83 of the [Mexican Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_Constitution "Mexican Constitution") limits the president to a single six-year term, called a _[sexenio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexenio_(Mexico) "Sexenio (Mexico)")_; no one who has served as president, even on a caretaker basis, may run for or serve in the office again.[&#8202;](#ref-back-33-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-33) The winner takes office on 1 October.[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-34)

### Congress

Main articles: [2024 Mexican Senate election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Mexican_Senate_election "2024 Mexican Senate election") and [2024 Mexican Chamber of Deputies election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Mexican_Chamber_of_Deputies_election "2024 Mexican Chamber of Deputies election")

The 500 members of the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_(Mexico) "Chamber of Deputies (Mexico)") serve three-year terms and are elected under a mixed system. Three hundred are chosen by plurality in [single-member districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_electoral_districts_of_Mexico "Federal electoral districts of Mexico"), and the remaining 200 by [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation") from regional party lists in five [electoral regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_regions_of_Mexico "Electoral regions of Mexico") of 40 seats each, with seats divided according to [Hamilton's method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Largest_remainders_method "Largest remainders method").[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto2-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-auto2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-auto1) The lists must observe gender parity and are headed alternately by women and men from one election to the next.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ine-rp-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-ine-rp) Those elected in 2024 form the [LXVI Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LXVI_Legislature_of_the_Mexican_Congress "LXVI Legislature of the Mexican Congress").[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-38)

Only parties that win at least 3% of the valid national vote take part in the allocation of proportional seats. Two limits restrict the result: no party may hold more than 300 seats under both principles combined, and no party's share of the chamber may exceed its share of the national vote by more than eight percentage points. A party that would breach either limit loses proportional seats until it complies, and the surplus seats pass to the other parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ine-rp-1)[\[35\]](#refnote-ine-rp) Coalition partners appear on the ballot under their own emblems and votes are counted for each party separately; since 2008 the electoral authority has applied the limits to each party rather than to a coalition as a whole.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-expansion) The INE allocates the proportional seats once the [Electoral Tribunal of the Federal Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_Tribunal_of_the_Federal_Judiciary "Electoral Tribunal of the Federal Judiciary") (TEPJF) has ruled on all challenges to the district results, and no later than 23 August.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ine-rp-2)[\[35\]](#refnote-ine-rp)

The 128 members of the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Republic_(Mexico) "Senate of the Republic (Mexico)") serve six-year terms. Ninety-six are elected in 32 three-seat constituencies based on the country's [states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/States_of_Mexico "States of Mexico"), and the remaining 32 in a single nationwide constituency by proportional representation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CEUM56-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-CEUM56) In the three-seat constituencies, two seats are allocated to the party or coalition receiving the most votes ( _mayoría relativa_)[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-41) and one seat to the party or coalition receiving the second-highest votes ( _primera minoría_).[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-42)[&#8202;](#ref-back-IPU-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-IPU)[&#8202;](#ref-back-SIL-congreso-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-SIL-congreso)

Under a 2014 constitutional reform, deputies may serve up to four consecutive terms and senators up to two. The 2024 election was the first at which sitting senators could seek a consecutive term.[&#8202;](#ref-back-excelsior-reeleccion-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-excelsior-reeleccion)

### Voters and administration

The [National Electoral Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_Electoral "Instituto Nacional Electoral") (INE) oversees federal elections in Mexico. Its responsibilities include organizing election day logistics, producing and distributing electoral materials, counting votes, and certifying the election results.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-46)

[![A person receives electoral ink on her thumb after casting her vote.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cb/Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_15.jpg/500px-Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_15.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_15.jpg) A person receives electoral ink on her thumb after casting her vote.

Voters must present their voter ID at polling stations to cast their ballots. Voters with physical limitations or disabilities and voters in preventive detention can vote in advance, with electoral materials sent to the voter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-:10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-:11) Mexican citizens residing abroad who maintain their political rights can vote by registering in the Electoral Roll for Citizens Residing Abroad and casting their votes at an embassy, electronically, or by mail.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-49) On 30 April 2024, the INE announced that there were 98,329,591 registered voters in Mexico, 187,388 registered voters abroad, 30,391 registered voters in preventive detention, and 4,002 registered for early voting.[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-50) It approved 170,182 polling stations nationwide.[&#8202;](#ref-back-51-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-51)

On election night, the INE publishes two sets of figures, both for information only. The Preliminary Electoral Results Program (PREP) progressively counts votes from polling-station tally sheets as they are delivered to the INE's district offices and publishes them online, while the quick count estimates turnout and each candidate's share from a random sample of polling stations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:192-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-:192)[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-53) The official result comes from the district tally, in which tally sheets are recorded at the 300 district offices under the oversight of citizen councils and party representatives; electoral packages may be reopened for a recount, and a certificate of majority is then issued to the winner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:192-1)[\[50\]](#refnote-:192) The validation of the presidential result and the declaration of the president-elect are the exclusive responsibility of the TEPJF.

### Redistricting

In December 2022, the INE redistributed the country's [300 electoral districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_electoral_districts_of_Mexico "Federal electoral districts of Mexico"), making this the first election with the new boundaries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-:5) Minor adjustments were also made to the [five multi-member constituencies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_regions_of_Mexico "Electoral regions of Mexico"), affecting the states of [Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hidalgo_(state) "Hidalgo (state)") and [Querétaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quer%C3%A9taro "Querétaro").[&#8202;](#ref-back-dof20230217-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-dof20230217)

#### Newly created seats

Seven new districts were created.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-1)[\[52\]](#refnote-:5)

1. [Baja California's 9th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_federal_electoral_district_of_Baja_California "9th federal electoral district of Baja California")

4. [Nuevo León's 14th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_federal_electoral_district_of_Nuevo_Le%C3%B3n "14th federal electoral district of Nuevo León")
5. [Puebla's 16th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_federal_electoral_district_of_Puebla "16th federal electoral district of Puebla")

#### Seats eliminated

Seven districts were eliminated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:5-2)[\[52\]](#refnote-:5)

1. [Mexico City's 23rd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/23rd_federal_electoral_district_of_Mexico_City "23rd federal electoral district of Mexico City")
2. [Mexico City's 24th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24th_federal_electoral_district_of_Mexico_City "24th federal electoral district of Mexico City")
3. [Guerrero's 9th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_federal_electoral_district_of_Guerrero "9th federal electoral district of Guerrero")
4. [State of Mexico's 41st](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/41st_federal_electoral_district_of_the_State_of_Mexico "41st federal electoral district of the State of Mexico")
5. [Michoacán's 12th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12th_federal_electoral_district_of_Michoac%C3%A1n "12th federal electoral district of Michoacán")
6. [Tamaulipas's 9th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9th_federal_electoral_district_of_Tamaulipas "9th federal electoral district of Tamaulipas")
7. [Veracruz's 20th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20th_federal_electoral_district_of_Veracruz "20th federal electoral district of Veracruz")

## Political parties and coalitions

See also: [List of political parties in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_political_parties_in_Mexico "List of political parties in Mexico")

Seven national political parties were registered with the INE and were eligible to participate in the federal elections: the [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_(Mexico) "National Action Party (Mexico)") (PAN), the [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party "Institutional Revolutionary Party") (PRI), the [Party of the Democratic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Democratic_Revolution "Party of the Democratic Revolution") (PRD), the [Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Party_(Mexico) "Labor Party (Mexico)") (PT), the [Ecologist Green Party of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecologist_Green_Party_of_Mexico "Ecologist Green Party of Mexico") (PVEM), [Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens'_Movement_(Mexico) "Citizens' Movement (Mexico)") (MC) and the [National Regeneration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morena_(political_party) "Morena (political party)") (Morena).[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-56)

The General Law of Political Parties stipulates that national political parties can form coalitions for elections by submitting a coalition agreement to the electoral authority. Parties cannot join coalitions in their first election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-:16) All parties were eligible to join an alliance in this election.

Parties failing to reach 3% of the popular vote in the presidential or legislative elections will lose their registration as a national political party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:16-1)[\[55\]](#refnote-:16)

### Sigamos Haciendo Historia

Main article: [Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia")

[Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia") ("Let's Keep Making History") was established on 19 November 2023 as the direct successor to [Juntos Hacemos Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juntos_Hacemos_Historia "Juntos Hacemos Historia"), encompassing the [National Regeneration Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Regeneration_Movement "National Regeneration Movement") (Morena), the [Labor Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_Party_(Mexico) "Labor Party (Mexico)") (PT), and the [Ecologist Green Party of Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecologist_Green_Party_of_Mexico "Ecologist Green Party of Mexico") (PVEM).[&#8202;](#ref-back-:20-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-:20)[&#8202;](#ref-back-59-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-59)[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-60)

### Fuerza y Corazón por México

Main article: [Fuerza y Corazón por México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuerza_y_Coraz%C3%B3n_por_M%C3%A9xico "Fuerza y Corazón por México")

On 26 June 2023, the leaders of the constituent parties of [Va por México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Va_por_M%C3%A9xico "Va por México"), a [big tent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_tent "Big tent") opposition coalition, announced the formation of Frente Amplio por México ("Broad Front for Mexico"). Comprising the [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_(Mexico) "National Action Party (Mexico)") (PAN), the [Institutional Revolutionary Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party "Institutional Revolutionary Party") (PRI), and the [Party of the Democratic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_of_the_Democratic_Revolution "Party of the Democratic Revolution") (PRD), the grouping was created to select a presidential nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-61-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-61)

On 29 August 2023, [Dante Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Delgado_Rannauro "Dante Delgado Rannauro"), the party leader of [Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens'_Movement_(Mexico) "Citizens' Movement (Mexico)"), ruled out joining Frente Amplio por México.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-63)

On 20 November 2023, the grouping registered as a coalition under the name [Fuerza y Corazón por México](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuerza_y_Coraz%C3%B3n_por_M%C3%A9xico "Fuerza y Corazón por México") ("Strength and Heart for Mexico"), composed of the same parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-64)

## Presidential candidates

### Sigamos Haciendo Historia

On 11 June 2023, [Juntos Hacemos Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juntos_Hacemos_Historia "Juntos Hacemos Historia"), the predecessor coalition to [Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia"), announced an internal selection process to select a presumptive presidential nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-65) To prevent the misuse of power, the alliance required prospective candidates to resign from their government positions before standing for nomination.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-:0) The selection process was based on five opinion polls, with four conducted by polling firms suggested by the candidates and one conducted by the coalition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-67) The polls were conducted from 28 August to 4 September.

[Marcelo Ebrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Ebrard "Marcelo Ebrard"), [secretary of foreign affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_Foreign_Affairs_(Mexico) "Secretary of Foreign Affairs (Mexico)"), was the first to register as a candidate, followed by [Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Sheinbaum "Claudia Sheinbaum"), [Head of Government of Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_mayors_of_Mexico_City "List of mayors of Mexico City"). Other candidates included [Adán Augusto López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad%C3%A1n_Augusto_L%C3%B3pez_Hern%C3%A1ndez "Adán Augusto López Hernández"), [Gerardo Fernández Noroña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerardo_Fern%C3%A1ndez_Noro%C3%B1a "Gerardo Fernández Noroña"), [Ricardo Monreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Monreal "Ricardo Monreal"), and [Manuel Velasco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuel_Velasco_Coello "Manuel Velasco Coello").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-:3) On 6 September 2023, Sheinbaum was declared the winner and was later confirmed as the presumptive nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-69)[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-70)

On 19 November 2023, the day Sigamos Haciendo Historia was officially registered as a coalition, it designated Sheinbaum as its presidential nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:20-1)[\[56\]](#refnote-:20) Sheinbaum formally registered her candidacy at the INE on 18 February 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-71)

**Nominee**

- [Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Sheinbaum "Claudia Sheinbaum"), [Head of Government of Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_government_of_Mexico_City "List of heads of government of Mexico City") (2018–2023) and Mayor of [Tlalpan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tlalpan "Tlalpan") (2015–2017)

### Fuerza y Corazón por México

Frente Amplio por México required prospective candidates to gather 150,000 signatures, including at least 1,000 signatures from 17 of the country's 32 states. The selection process then included a series of polls to narrow the field, with the first one, conducted from 11 to 14 August, aimed to identify the top three candidates. A second poll was scheduled for 27–30 August, contributing 50% of the points needed for nomination, with the remaining 50% determined through an online survey on 3 September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-73)

Thirty-three individuals registered for the race, but only thirteen were approved by the grouping. Of those, four candidates successfully gathered the required signatures: [Xóchitl Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%B3chitl_G%C3%A1lvez_Ruiz "Xóchitl Gálvez Ruiz"), [Beatriz Paredes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatriz_Paredes_Rangel "Beatriz Paredes Rangel"), [Santiago Creel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Creel "Santiago Creel"), and [Enrique de la Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_de_la_Madrid_Cordero "Enrique de la Madrid Cordero").[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-74)[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-75) During the first poll, Enrique de la Madrid was eliminated. On 21 August, just before the second poll, Santiago Creel withdrew and endorsed Xóchitl Gálvez. In the second poll, Gálvez defeated Paredes by 15 points. On 31 August, the PRI officially endorsed Gálvez, effectively withdrawing its support for Paredes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-76) Later that day, the third poll was canceled, and Gálvez was confirmed as the presumptive presidential nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-77)

On 20 November 2023, the day Frente Amplio por México registered as a coalition under the name Fuerza y Corazón por México, it designated Gálvez as the coalition's presidential nominee.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-78) She formally registered her candidacy at the INE on 20 February 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-79)

|  | [Enrique de la Madrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_de_la_Madrid_Cordero "Enrique de la Madrid Cordero") | 15.6 | _Eliminated_ |

**Nominee**

- [Xóchitl Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%B3chitl_G%C3%A1lvez_Ruiz "Xóchitl Gálvez Ruiz"), [Senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Republic_(Mexico) "Senate of the Republic (Mexico)") (2018–2023) and Mayor of [Miguel Hidalgo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miguel_Hidalgo,_Mexico_City "Miguel Hidalgo, Mexico City") (2015–2018).

### Citizens' Movement

Citizens' Movement's presidential candidate registration period was open from 3 to 12 November. The first person to register was Senator [Indira Kempis Martínez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indira_Kempis_Mart%C3%ADnez "Indira Kempis Martínez"), who had previously declared her intention to run for president. Seven others, including [Samuel García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Garc%C3%ADa_(politician) "Samuel García (politician)"), governor of [Nuevo León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuevo_Le%C3%B3n "Nuevo León"), registered in the race.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-83)[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84) On 12 November, [Marcelo Ebrard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Ebrard "Marcelo Ebrard"), who had failed to be selected as the candidate for [Sigamos Haciendo Historia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sigamos_Haciendo_Historia "Sigamos Haciendo Historia"), announced he was not seeking the party's nomination despite being courted by the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ebrard-MC-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-Ebrard-MC)

On 17 November, the party disqualified seven out of eight candidates, leaving only Samuel García.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-:1) However, on 2 December, García suspended his campaign due to a [political crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%932025_Nuevo_Le%C3%B3n_political_crisis#Gubernatorial_succession_crisis_(October%E2%80%93December_2023) "2022–2025 Nuevo León political crisis") in his state over appointing an interim governor to replace him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-87) On 9 January 2024, federal deputy [Jorge Máynez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_M%C3%A1ynez "Jorge Máynez") was nominated as a substitute for Samuel García, with García announcing on social media that Máynez would be the party's next presidential candidate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-88) Máynez was officially designated as the party's presidential nominee the next day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-89) Máynez formally registered his candidacy at the INE on 22 February 2024.[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-90)

**Nominee**

- [Jorge Máynez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_M%C3%A1ynez "Jorge Máynez"), federal deputy (2021–2024, 2015–2018) and deputy of the [Congress of Zacatecas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Zacatecas "Congress of Zacatecas") (2010–2013).

### Independents

The registration deadline for individuals wishing to run for president as independent candidates (i.e., without the backing of a registered party) expired on 7 September 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-91)

To formalize their candidacies, independent presidential hopefuls must collect the signatures of voters endorsing them in an amount equal to 1% of the country's entire electoral roll – a total of 961,405[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-92) – distributed equally across at least 17 of the nation's states within 120 days.[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93)

A total of 27 individuals informed the INE of their wish to run for the presidency as independent candidates before the deadline. By 7 September, six of them had been permitted to begin collecting signatures; the remaining 21 were given 48 hours to correct shortcomings in the documentation they had presented.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27_aspirantes-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-27_aspirantes) The six green-lighted prospective independent candidates were Rocío Gabriela González Castañeda, [Ulises Ernesto Ruiz Ortiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulises_Ernesto_Ruiz_Ortiz "Ulises Ernesto Ruiz Ortiz"), César Enrique Asiain del Castillo, [Hugo Eric Flores Cervantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Eric_Flores_Cervantes "Hugo Eric Flores Cervantes"), María Ofelia Edgar Mares, and [José Eduardo Verástegui Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Ver%C3%A1stegui "Eduardo Verástegui").[&#8202;](#ref-back-27_aspirantes-1)[\[92\]](#refnote-27_aspirantes) A further three – Fernando Mauricio Jiménez Chávez, Manuel Antonio Romo Aguirre, and Ignacio Benavente Torres – were announced on 27 September.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-95)[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-96)

After the 120-day deadline, the INE announced that no prospective independent candidates had successfully collected the required signatures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-97)[&#8202;](#ref-back-98-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-98)

## Campaigns

The INE established that the campaigning period for president, senators, and federal deputies would officially begin on 1 March and conclude on 29 May, three days before the elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-99) [Election silence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_silence "Election silence") was observed from 30 May until the polls closed on 2 June.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-100)

### Campaign finance

Under the INE's financing plan for 2024, the national parties were to receive MXN10.4billion in public funding, of which MXN3.3billion was earmarked for campaigning; Morena's share, MXN3.2billion, was about 30% of the total.[&#8202;](#ref-back-101-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-101) The INE capped spending at MXN660.9million for each presidential campaign, up from MXN429.6million in 2018,[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-102) and at about MXN2.2million for each Chamber of Deputies race; caps for Senate races varied by state, from MXN4.4million to MXN44.1million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-103-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-103) Parties could not buy broadcast advertising; instead the INE allocated about 30.5million radio and television spots to them across the precampaign and campaign periods, 30% divided equally and 70% according to each party's vote in the previous Chamber of Deputies election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-104)

### Presidential campaign

#### Launch and opening weeks

The three candidates opened their campaigns on 1 March. Gálvez began shortly after midnight in [Fresnillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresnillo "Fresnillo"), Zacatecas, where she walked with families of victims of violence, and later that day in [Irapuato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irapuato "Irapuato") signed a pledge in her own blood not to abolish the government's social programs. Sheinbaum spoke before thousands of supporters in Mexico City's [Zócalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%B3calo "Zócalo"), and Máynez opened his campaign that night before a small crowd in [Lagos de Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagos_de_Moreno "Lagos de Moreno"), Jalisco.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-arranque-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-expansion-arranque) Sheinbaum entered the race more than 20 percentage points ahead of Gálvez, with Máynez a distant third at about 5%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters-arranca-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-reuters-arranca)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/99/Inicio_de_campa%C3%B1a_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum.jpg/500px-Inicio_de_campa%C3%B1a_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Inicio_de_campa%C3%B1a_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum.jpg) Sheinbaum opening her campaign in Mexico City’s Zócalo on 1 March 2024

Sheinbaum ran on continuity. At the Zócalo, she presented a 100-point program that kept López Obrador's social programs and flagship projects, and asked voters to help build the "second floor of the [transformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Transformation "Fourth Transformation")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-107) Analysts described her offer as "total continuity", designed to avoid any distance from a president whose supporters she relied on.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-108) Her team was coordinated by Morena's president, [Mario Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mario_Delgado_(politician) "Mario Delgado (politician)"), and included three of her rivals for the nomination, [Adán Augusto López](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ad%C3%A1n_Augusto_L%C3%B3pez "Adán Augusto López"), [Ricardo Monreal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Monreal "Ricardo Monreal") and [Gerardo Fernández Noroña](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerardo_Fern%C3%A1ndez_Noro%C3%B1a "Gerardo Fernández Noroña"); Delgado set the party the task of placing representatives at every polling station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-109) She campaigned mainly through rallies: in the first month she visited 21 states and held 60 rallies, covering 72 of the 300 electoral districts, about half of them in states governed by the opposition; the opposition criticized her for skipping private-sector forums. Gálvez visited 19 states in the same period, mostly at indoor events.[&#8202;](#ref-back-milenio-mes-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-milenio-mes)

Gálvez spent her first weeks campaigning mainly on security.[&#8202;](#ref-back-111-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-111)[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-debate2-preview-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-exp-debate2-preview) Three weeks into the campaign she reorganized her team, appointing Max Cortázar deputy coordinator in charge of messaging and advertising and reducing the role of her coordinator, [Santiago Creel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santiago_Creel "Santiago Creel"), who remained her link to the parties and was negotiating with them for more money and broadcast time.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-113)

Máynez built his campaign around voters aged 18 to 29, who made up 27% of the electorate, visiting a university a day in its first weeks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-114-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-114) His jingle "Presidente Máynez" spread widely on social media, where young users posted dance routines to it; by April he had 1.7 million followers on [TikTok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TikTok "TikTok"), against Gálvez's 503,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-115-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-115)

#### Mid-campaign

López Obrador's daily press conferences became a recurring point of dispute. In late March, the INE's complaints commission ordered the removal or editing of content from five of them, while declining to suspend the conferences; councillor Claudia Zavala said that it was the constitution, not the INE, that imposed a duty of neutrality on public servants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-116-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-116) The commission ordered four further conferences modified in April,[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-117) and in early May, the removal of one in which López Obrador had accused the INE of foul play in organizing the debates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-118-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-118)

Ahead of the second of the three presidential debates, in late April, Sheinbaum, who had visited 29 of the 32 states, said that "a debate can hardly change that difference", and set out to defend her lead and López Obrador's record.[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-debate2-preview-1)[\[110\]](#refnote-exp-debate2-preview) Gálvez had by then shifted her message from security to reassuring voters that "the social programs stay and Morena goes", after meeting the leaders of the PAN, PRI and PRD;[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-debate2-preview-2)[\[110\]](#refnote-exp-debate2-preview) at a closed-door meeting in mid-April, she and the party leaders agreed to unify the coalition's message around her.[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-119) The political scientist Patricio Morales judged that she had not managed to communicate her project.[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-debate2-preview-3)[\[110\]](#refnote-exp-debate2-preview)

With Gálvez trailing, her allies urged Máynez to withdraw and his supporters to cast a [tactical vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tactical_vote "Tactical vote") for her. After the second debate, PRI president [Alejandro Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Moreno_C%C3%A1rdenas "Alejandro Moreno Cárdenas") and PAN president [Marko Cortés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marko_Antonio_Cort%C3%A9s_Mendoza "Marko Antonio Cortés Mendoza") asked him to step aside, and the businessman Claudio X. González joined the call days later.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-declina-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-expansion-declina) Moreno offered Máynez the leadership of the Mexican Youth Institute (IMJUVE) and called him a "scab" working for Sheinbaum.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-121) On 14 May, he went further, proposing to resign the party leadership and give up his Senate candidacy if Máynez withdrew before the final debate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-122) Cortés, accepting that Máynez would stay in the race, urged voters to back the candidate able to defeat "a populist and destructive government".[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-divididos-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-expansion-divididos) Gálvez declined to seek a deal with Máynez but said she would court his voters directly.[&#8202;](#ref-back-124-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-124)

Máynez refused each time. He answered that the opposition parties should withdraw in his favor instead and dismissed Moreno's offer as a sign of desperation;[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-declina-1)[\[118\]](#refnote-expansion-declina)[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-125) MC leader [Dante Delgado](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Delgado_Rannauro "Dante Delgado Rannauro") called the claim that his party split the opposition vote a fallacy of the "old politics".[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-divididos-1)[\[121\]](#refnote-expansion-divididos) Máynez also released a new version of his jingle urging supporters to vote for him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-126-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-126) Sheinbaum, who had said that Máynez had "every right to take part", mocked her opponents for "fighting over who resigns".[&#8202;](#ref-back-127-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-127)[&#8202;](#ref-back-128-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-128)

#### Final weeks

On 22 May, a [stage collapsed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Pedro_Garza_Garc%C3%ADa_stage_collapse "San Pedro Garza García stage collapse") in high winds at a Máynez rally in [San Pedro Garza García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Pedro_Garza_Garc%C3%ADa "San Pedro Garza García"), Nuevo León, killing at least nine people and injuring more than 120. Máynez suspended his campaign events, and Sheinbaum cancelled a rally in nearby [Monterrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monterrey "Monterrey") the next day.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-129)

Polls differed on how far Gálvez had closed the gap. _El Financiero_'s series showed her moving from 17 points behind Sheinbaum at the start of the campaign to 10 points behind at its end,[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-130) while the Associated Press reported that her popularity had been declining.[&#8202;](#ref-back-131-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-131) Sheinbaum told supporters in mid-May not to be complacent despite her lead, saying that Morena and its allies now had representatives covering every polling station.[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-132)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/Cierre_de_campa%C3%B1a_presidencial_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum_1.jpg/500px-Cierre_de_campa%C3%B1a_presidencial_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum_1.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Cierre_de_campa%C3%B1a_presidencial_de_Claudia_Sheinbaum_1.jpg) Aerial view of the Zócalo during Sheinbaum’s closing rally on 29 May 2024

The candidates closed their campaigns on 29 May. Sheinbaum returned to the Zócalo, saying she had visited more than 170 municipalities and held more than 160 rallies;[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-cierre-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-expansion-cierre) she stepped back from her confrontations with Gálvez, offering "peace and harmony" to all Mexicans and pledging to safeguard López Obrador's legacy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-134) Gálvez held her main closing rally at the [Arena Monterrey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arena_Monterrey "Arena Monterrey") and a final event in her hometown of Tepatepec, Hidalgo, and Máynez closed with a music festival at the Auditorio BlackBerry in Mexico City.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-cierre-1)[\[131\]](#refnote-expansion-cierre)

### Legislative campaign

Candidate selection drew criticism in both blocs. Morena chose its district candidates through surveys, which missed their January deadline,[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-135) and one of its senators, José Ramón Enríquez, accused party president Mario Delgado of assigning candidacies through "political agreements" without surveys;[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-136) in February, the party filled most places on its proportional-representation lists by lottery.[&#8202;](#ref-back-137-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-137) The leaders of the PAN, PRI and PRD – [Marko Cortés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marko_Cort%C3%A9s "Marko Cortés"), [Alejandro Moreno](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alejandro_Moreno_C%C3%A1rdenas "Alejandro Moreno Cárdenas"), and [Jesús Zambrano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jes%C3%BAs_Zambrano_Grijalva "Jesús Zambrano Grijalva") – each placed themselves first on their party's Senate proportional representation list,[&#8202;](#ref-back-138-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-138) which former president [Felipe Calderón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felipe_Calder%C3%B3n "Felipe Calderón") called "vulgar ambition" and a burden for Gálvez.[&#8202;](#ref-back-139-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-139)

Morena and its allies campaigned for a two-thirds majority in both chambers, enough to amend the constitution without opposition votes: 334 of 500 seats in the Chamber of Deputies and 86 of 128 in the Senate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuters-arranca-1)[\[104\]](#refnote-reuters-arranca) Analysts saw the congressional contest as the decisive one for the next government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-wilson-congress-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-wilson-congress) The goal was to pass the package of constitutional reforms known as "Plan C". At a rally in [San Luis Potosí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Luis_Potos%C3%AD "San Luis Potosí") on 6 March, Sheinbaum asked voters to back the coalition's parties in every race so that "all judges are elected by the people of Mexico".[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-planc-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-expansion-planc) The opposition presented the contest as a defense of democratic institutions, and Sheinbaum used her closing speech to reject the framing that the election was a choice between "democracy or authoritarianism".[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-cierre-2)[\[131\]](#refnote-expansion-cierre)

### Campaign issues

#### Security

In _[El Financiero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_Financiero "El Financiero")_'s polling, public security remained the most urgent problem a month into the campaign.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ef-april-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-ef-april) Intentional homicides under López Obrador had peaked at 36,773 in 2020 and fell to 29,675 in 2023, still above any year between 1990 and 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-:6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-144) His government had pursued a strategy summed up by the slogan " [hugs, not bullets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrazos,_no_balazos "Abrazos, no balazos")" and created the [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_(Mexico) "National Guard (Mexico)").

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Guardia_Nacional_de_M%C3%A9xico.jpg/500px-Guardia_Nacional_de_M%C3%A9xico.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Guardia_Nacional_de_M%C3%A9xico.jpg) National Guard officers in 2023; the force’s future was a central issue of the campaign

Gálvez made the issue the center of her challenge. At a rally in Irapuato on the first day of the campaign, she declared that "hugs for criminals are over" and promised a much more aggressive approach, including doubling the National Guard to 300,000 members under civilian command.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ap-begin-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-ap-begin) She also proposed higher pay for state police, a national police university, more prosecutors and judges, a new maximum-security prison, and closer cooperation with the United States against the cartels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-146)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-:7) For Sheinbaum, _[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy "Foreign Policy")_ noted, openly criticizing López Obrador's record was risky because she relied on his supporters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp-security-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-fp-security) She offered continuity with a technical emphasis drawn from her time as [Head of Government of Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_Government_of_Mexico_City "Head of Government of Mexico City"), where homicides had fallen to their lowest level since 1989: "zero impunity" through better intelligence and closer coordination between the National Guard, state police and prosecutors.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-1)[\[145\]](#refnote-:7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-:8)

The issue was also fought through accusations of links to organized crime. A January 2024 report by _[ProPublica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ProPublica "ProPublica")_ alleging that traffickers had funded López Obrador's 2006 campaign set off the hashtag #NarcoPresidente and variants aimed at Sheinbaum, which the government attributed to bot accounts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-150) After Gálvez called Sheinbaum a "narco-candidate" in the second debate, the INE's complaints commission ordered the remarks removed as potentially defamatory;[&#8202;](#ref-back-151-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-151) Gálvez repeated the term at a rally days later,[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-152) and such accusations dominated the final debate, where Sheinbaum answered that the drug war launched by Calderón had meant "permission to kill".[&#8202;](#ref-back-153-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-153) On 24 May, the INE also barred Gálvez from calling Morena a "narco-party".[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-154)

Commentators noted that López Obrador remained popular although his security policy had failed to curb killings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian-amlo-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-guardian-amlo)

#### Social programs

López Obrador's government had expanded cash transfers, the largest being the Pension for the Well-being of Older People ( _Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores_) for everyone aged 65 and over,[&#8202;](#ref-back-156-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-156)[&#8202;](#ref-back-157-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-157) and the programs divided the electorate. In _El Financiero_'s March poll, 53% of respondents lived in households that received one; Sheinbaum led Gálvez by 64% to 21% among them, while Gálvez led by 47% to 36% among everyone else.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ef-april-1)[\[140\]](#refnote-ef-april)

On her first day of campaigning, Gálvez signed a pledge in her own blood that no program would be abolished, and promised to lower the pension age to 60.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-158) She argued that fear of losing the programs was what favored Morena, and accused the government's door-to-door program staff of telling beneficiaries that a vote for another party would cost them their benefits;[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-159) in Mexico City she campaigned under the slogan "Morena goes, the social programs stay".[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-sevan-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-exp-sevan) Sheinbaum replied that the programs were "rights of the people of Mexico", not handouts, that only her movement could guarantee them, and that the PAN had voted against the universal pension.[&#8202;](#ref-back-161-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-161) She pledged that payments would always rise faster than inflation and proposed two new programs, scholarships for all pupils in basic education, and a payment for women aged 60 to 64.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:8-1)[\[147\]](#refnote-:8)[&#8202;](#ref-back-162-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-162) Máynez also promised to keep the programs, focusing instead on better-paid work,[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-maynez-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-expansion-maynez) including a cut in the legal working week from 48 to 40 hours, which Sheinbaum said she would study and Gálvez said was premature.[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-164)

#### Health

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/90/ProtestasContraElDesabastoMedicamentoVIH20200221_ohs01.jpg/500px-ProtestasContraElDesabastoMedicamentoVIH20200221_ohs01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ProtestasContraElDesabastoMedicamentoVIH20200221_ohs01.jpg) A 2020 protest over shortages of HIV medicines

The three campaigns agreed that the priorities were ending medicine shortages and widening coverage, but differed on the remedy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-salud-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-expansion-salud) Sheinbaum defended the government's centralized model, in which IMSS-Bienestar serves people without social security and the state-owned Birmex buys medicines in bulk, adding contracts with private pharmacies to dispense free generics.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-salud-1)[\[163\]](#refnote-expansion-salud)[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-166) Gálvez campaigned on the shortages, promising to restore medicine distribution within four months and to revive the _Seguro Popular_, with a "Mi Salud" card allowing treatment in public or private hospitals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-salud-2)[\[163\]](#refnote-expansion-salud)[&#8202;](#ref-back-exp-sevan-1)[\[158\]](#refnote-exp-sevan)[&#8202;](#ref-back-exc-debate1-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-exc-debate1) Máynez proposed a universal system regardless of employment status.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-maynez-1)[\[161\]](#refnote-expansion-maynez)

#### Plan C and democratic institutions

López Obrador coined "Plan C" in 2023, when the Supreme Court appeared set to strike down his "Plan B" electoral reform: if the courts blocked his changes, voters should give his movement the 334 deputies needed to amend the constitution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-expansion-planc-explainer-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-expansion-planc-explainer) On 5 February 2024, he sent Congress twenty constitutional reforms, including the popular election of judges and electoral authorities, a smaller INE, and the abolition of all seats allocated by [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proportional_representation "Proportional representation"), which would cut the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_(Mexico) "Chamber of Deputies (Mexico)") from 500 to 300 seats and the [Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_of_the_Republic_(Mexico) "Senate of the Republic (Mexico)") from 128 to 64.[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-169) As early as October 2023, Sheinbaum urged Morena’s supporters to win the two-thirds majorities that Plan C required.[&#8202;](#ref-back-170-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-170) During the campaign she backed the popular election of judges, Supreme Court justices and INE councillors,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:7-2)[\[145\]](#refnote-:7)[&#8202;](#ref-back-171-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-171) the abolition of proportional-representation seats,[&#8202;](#ref-back-172-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-172) and a constitutional ban on re-election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-173-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-173)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Marea_rosa.jpg/500px-Marea_rosa.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Marea_rosa.jpg) A _Marea Rosa_ march in 2023

The opposition made the defence of democratic institutions its central theme. The constituent parties of _Fuerza y Corazón por México_ had blocked earlier attempts at electoral reform, and a civic movement known as the _Marea Rosa_ (“Pink Tide”) had formed in late 2022 to defend the INE from López Obrador’s proposed reforms. On 18 February 2024, its “march for democracy” filled Mexico City’s Zócalo, with the government estimating 90,000 attendees and organizers about 700,000.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-174) At the final debate, Gálvez warned that democracy was at risk and that Supreme Court justices should not be chosen by “quotas or cronies”; Máynez agreed the judiciary needed reform, but not one that undermined its autonomy or concentrated power.[&#8202;](#ref-back-zeta-debate3-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-zeta-debate3)

The message reached a limited audience. Shortly before the vote, 15% of respondents told _El Financiero_ they identified with the _Marea Rosa_ movement, against 49% who did not; Gálvez led by two to one among the first group and Sheinbaum by almost three to one among the second.[&#8202;](#ref-back-176-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-176) The historian Humberto Beck judged the opposition's criticisms "in one sense correct", but said its own democratic credentials were far from impeccable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-guardian-amlo-1)[\[153\]](#refnote-guardian-amlo)

#### Energy

The two leading candidates backed a transition to cleaner energy but by different routes:[&#8202;](#ref-back-de-energia-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-de-energia) Sheinbaum proposed continuing López Obrador's "energy sovereignty" policy of strengthening [Pemex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pemex "Pemex") and the [CFE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Electricity_Commission "Federal Electricity Commission"), while investing US$13.6billion in renewables by 2030 and allowing more private participation than her predecessor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-de-energia-1)[\[175\]](#refnote-de-energia) Gálvez proposed reviving the market opening of the 2013 energy reform, including renewable-energy auctions and oil tenders, and opening Pemex to private investment on the model of Brazil's [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras "Petrobras").[&#8202;](#ref-back-de-energia-2)[\[175\]](#refnote-de-energia)[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-178) Máynez proposed a national clean-energy model to "move this country with the energy of the sun and the wind".[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-179)

### Debates

Before the campaigning period, the INE set the date and venue for the three presidential debates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-180) According to electoral law, presidential candidates are required to take part in a minimum of two debates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-181) The INE chose the moderators about a month before each debate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-182)[&#8202;](#ref-back-183-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-183)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ine-mod3-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-ine-mod3) All debates were held in [Mexico City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_City "Mexico City").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:42-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-:42)

| Date | Time | Venue | Moderator(s) | Participants | Viewership<br>(millions) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 7 April 2024 | 8:00p.m. [CST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Time_Zone "Central Time Zone") | [Instituto Nacional Electoral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instituto_Nacional_Electoral "Instituto Nacional Electoral") | [Denise Maerker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denise_Maerker "Denise Maerker")<br>Manuel López San Martín | [Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Sheinbaum "Claudia Sheinbaum")<br>[Xóchitl Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%B3chitl_G%C3%A1lvez "Xóchitl Gálvez")<br>[Jorge Máynez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_M%C3%A1ynez "Jorge Máynez") | 13.7[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-186) |
| 28 April 2024 | 8:00p.m. [CST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Time_Zone "Central Time Zone") | [Estudios Churubusco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estudios_Churubusco "Estudios Churubusco") | Adriana Pérez Cañedo<br>Alejandro Cacho | 16.18[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-187) |  |
| 19 May 2024 | 8:00p.m. [CST](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Time_Zone "Central Time Zone") | [Centro Cultural Universitario Tlatelolco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro_Cultural_Universitario_Tlatelolco?action=edit&redlink=1 "Centro Cultural Universitario Tlatelolco")\[ [es](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centro%20Cultural%20Universitario%20Tlatelolco "es:Centro Cultural Universitario Tlatelolco")\] ( [UNAM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico "National Autonomous University of Mexico")) | Luisa Cantú Ríos<br>Elena Solís<br>[Javier Solórzano Zinser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier_Sol%C3%B3rzano_Zinser "Javier Solórzano Zinser")\[ [es](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Javier%20Sol%C3%B3rzano "es:Javier Solórzano")\] | 13.9[&#8202;](#ref-back-188-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-188) |  |

Debates for the 2024 Mexican presidential election

The first debate was held on 7 April 2024 at the INE's headquarters. The theme was "the society we want", with questions on health, education, corruption, discrimination and violence against women.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-:4) The Associated Press described it as light on proposals and heavy on personal attacks.[&#8202;](#ref-back-190-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-190) According to _El País_, Gálvez attacked Sheinbaum at every opportunity,[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-191) raising the collapse of the Colegio Rébsamen in the [2017 earthquake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Puebla_earthquake "2017 Puebla earthquake"), the [Mexico City Metro overpass collapse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexico_City_Metro_overpass_collapse "Mexico City Metro overpass collapse") and the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Mexico "COVID-19 pandemic in Mexico") in Mexico City, and calling her the "ice lady"; Máynez accused both of belonging to the "old politics".[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-auto)

The fact-checkers of the news site _Animal Político_ found false statements from all three candidates. Sheinbaum said that femicides had fallen by more than 40% nationally, when official figures showed a fall of 7.5% between 2018 and 2023.[&#8202;](#ref-back-193-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-193) Gálvez said that 70% of children did not understand what they read, when the OECD's PISA tests put the share at 53%, and Máynez gave false figures on medicine vouchers under the [Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto "Enrique Peña Nieto") administration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-194) Commentators saw no clear winner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-195) López Obrador complained that "the whole narrative of the debate was not to recognize anything" done under his administration.[&#8202;](#ref-back-196-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-196)

Election specialists faulted the INE's organization of the first debate: the on-screen clock malfunctioned, candidates had about a minute and a half to answer on subjects such as health, and the analyst Georgina de la Fuente judged that the INE had been the debate's loser.[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-197) Gálvez called the event a "disaster" and said the format differed from the one the campaigns had agreed, which had included a segment for direct exchanges between the candidates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-198) On 11 April, INE president [Guadalupe Taddei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalupe_Taddei "Guadalupe Taddei") accepted the criticism and said the institute would act on it for the next debate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-199-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-199) Five days later, representatives of the three campaigns met the INE's debates commission and asked for written agreements and for the moderators to keep to the agreed questions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-200)

The second debate, on 28 April 2024 at [Estudios Churubusco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Estudios_Churubusco "Estudios Churubusco"), addressed "the route to the development of Mexico", covering the economy, infrastructure, poverty and climate change, with questions gathered from citizens in all 32 states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[187\]](#refnote-:4) According to the INE, it was the most watched debate in Mexican history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-201) Gálvez pressed Sheinbaum on alleged corruption in López Obrador's administration, including allegations against one of the president's sons and accusations of [illicit enrichment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illicit_enrichment "Illicit enrichment") against [Rocío Nahle García](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roc%C3%ADo_Nahle_Garc%C3%ADa "Rocío Nahle García"); Sheinbaum challenged her to file a complaint.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-202) The two traded insults, Sheinbaum calling Gálvez "the corrupt one" and Gálvez calling Sheinbaum the "narco-candidate".[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-203) Sheinbaum and Gálvez both pledged to keep López Obrador's social programs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-204) _Animal Político_ again found errors from all three candidates. Sheinbaum's statement that "there is no inflation" was false, and other figures she used to defend the government's record were false or misleading.[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-205) Gálvez’s statements that the country was suffering the worst drought in its history and that she had won her government contracts under Morena through open tenders were both false.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-207) Máynez used more precise data than either rival, although some of his figures were inexact.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-208)

The third and final debate was held on 19 May 2024 at [UNAM's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Autonomous_University_of_Mexico "National Autonomous University of Mexico") Centro Cultural Universitario Tlatelolco. The theme was "democracy and government: constructive dialogues", covering social policy, crime, migration, foreign policy and the division of powers. The INE had planned a segment in which the candidates would question each other directly,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:43-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-:43) but on 9 May, the campaigns agreed instead to submit questions for the moderators to choose from and ask.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-210) Sheinbaum defended López Obrador's approach to the [Mexican drug war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mexican_drug_war "Mexican drug war"), Gálvez pledged to strengthen the [National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Guard_(Mexico) "National Guard (Mexico)") and the police, and Máynez concentrated on young people, promising a five-day work week and more university places.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-211) Defending mandatory pretrial detention, Sheinbaum said that abolishing it would free 60,000 criminals; _Animal Político_ found that about 44,000 people were held under it, none of them convicted. Máynez's statement that Mexico had the ninth-largest number of poor people in the world was also false, and several of Gálvez's figures were misleading or debatable, though none of those checked was false.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-212) Commentators criticized the loss of the face-to-face segment, saying it hindered real exchange,[&#8202;](#ref-back-:23-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-:23) and were divided on the winner.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:23-1)[\[211\]](#refnote-:23)[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-214)

Carin Zissis of the [Americas Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Americas_Society "Americas Society")/ [Council of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_the_Americas "Council of the Americas") wrote that many observers expected the debates to have little effect on the result unless a candidate performed disastrously.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-215)

## Conduct

[![A voter in a wheelchair at a lowered voting booth.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_16.jpg/500px-Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_16.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Elecciones_federales_de_M%C3%A9xico_de_2024_16.jpg) Voter in a wheelchair uses an adapted voting booth.

Early voting for voters with disabilities or limited physical mobility and those in preventive detention was held from 6 to 20 May.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:10-1)[\[45\]](#refnote-:10)[&#8202;](#ref-back-:11-1)[\[46\]](#refnote-:11) On 2 June, polling stations opened at 8:00 CST and closed at 18:00 CST.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-216) By 20:00 that evening, the INE reported that all but 23 of the approved polling stations had been installed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-217)

### Election day violence

On 1 June, authorities ordered the suspension of voting in the municipalities of [Pantelhó](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantelh%C3%B3 "Pantelhó") and [Chicomuselo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicomuselo "Chicomuselo") in [Chiapas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiapas "Chiapas"), citing the burning of election papers in the former by unknown individuals on 31 May and threats against poll workers by gang members.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-218) On election day, two people were killed in shootings at polling stations at [Coyomeapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coyomeapan "Coyomeapan") and [Tlanalapan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tlanalapan?action=edit&redlink=1 "Tlanalapan")\[ [es](https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/San%20Rafael%20Tlanalapan "es:San Rafael Tlanalapan")\] in [Puebla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puebla "Puebla").[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-219)

## Results

About 60 million votes were cast in each election, with most of them being cast on election day. The election saw a lower turnout than the [2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Mexican_general_election "2012 Mexican general election") and [2018 presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Mexican_general_election "2018 Mexican general election"), at 61.05%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-220) [Claudia Sheinbaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claudia_Sheinbaum "Claudia Sheinbaum") received 35.9 million votes, surpassing [López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador "Andrés Manuel López Obrador")'s 2018 record of 30.1 million and making her the candidate with the most votes in Mexican history.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:13-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-:13) She also achieved the highest vote percentage of any candidate since [1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_Mexican_general_election "1982 Mexican general election").

Sheinbaum carried 31 out of 32 states, with [Aguascalientes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aguascalientes "Aguascalientes") being the only state where a plurality voted for [Xóchitl Gálvez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/X%C3%B3chitl_G%C3%A1lvez "Xóchitl Gálvez").[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-221) Sheinbaum managed to flip [Guanajuato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guanajuato "Guanajuato") – the home state of former president [Vicente Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicente_Fox "Vicente Fox") – and a state considered a bastion for the [National Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Action_Party_(Mexico) "National Action Party (Mexico)") since 2000; it was the only state not voting for López Obrador in 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-222)

Besides Aguascalientes, Gálvez placed second in all states except [Campeche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campeche "Campeche"), where [Jorge Máynez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_M%C3%A1ynez "Jorge Máynez") defeated her by five and a half points.[&#8202;](#ref-back-223-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-223) She also obtained 5.4 million fewer votes than the combined total of both [Ricardo Anaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ricardo_Anaya "Ricardo Anaya") and [José Antonio Meade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Antonio_Meade "José Antonio Meade"), the PAN and PRI nominees for president in 2018, respectively; Gálvez obtained 16.5 million in total, while Anaya and Meade obtained 21.9 million votes combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-224)

Máynez's performance in the election marked the best result for [Citizens' Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Citizens'_Movement_(Mexico) "Citizens' Movement (Mexico)") in its history. The party received 5.2 million more votes compared to 2018 when it received about 1 million – about a 500% increase. Citizens' Movement secured the third-highest votes among all parties in the election, behind Morena and the PAN.[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-225)[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-226)
